Even when I’m supposed to just sit still and listen, I feel like there’s this motor running under my skin. I’ll want to get up, check something, or move on to the next thing right away. It’s hard to just sit there calmly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I’m having a really hard time keeping my mind on things long enough to finish them. It’s getting to the point where I can’t just push through it like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Yes. I don’t always stop to think first.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things you're supposed to do during the day?\n\nPatient: Yes, pretty often. I’ll forget normal but important things during the day—like returning a client’s call, paying the cloud backup bill, or bringing the right adapter to a volunteer class. My mother still jokes about that. She says my grade school report cards always said I was bright, but I wouldn’t stay in my seat, I’d blurt out answers, and I’d lose my homework even when I had actually done it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble managing your emotions, like getting really angry or upset for what seems like no reason?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I get frustrated like anyone, but I haven’t noticed myself getting really angry or upset for no reason in a way that stands out.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of inattention, impulsivity, and restlessness your whole life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it seems like it goes way back. Like I mentioned, my mother still jokes that when I was in grade school my report cards said I was bright, but I wouldn’t stay in my seat, I’d blurt out answers, and I’d lose homework even when I’d actually done it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I’m having a really hard time keeping my mind on things long enough to finish them. It’s getting to the point where I can’t just push through it like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Yes. Even when I’m supposed to just sit still and listen, I feel like there’s this motor running under my skin. I’ll want to get up, check something, or move on to the next thing right away. It’s hard to just sit there calmly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. In tech discussions especially, I’ll cut people off even when I know I shouldn’t. The answer pops into my head and I just start talking over them, or sometimes I’ll even reach for the keyboard before they’ve finished explaining the problem. I don’t always stop to think first.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things you're supposed to do during the day?\n\nPatient: Yes, pretty often. I’ll forget normal but important things during the day—like returning a client’s call, paying the cloud backup bill, or bringing the right adapter to a volunteer class. My mother still jokes about that. She says my grade school report cards always said I was bright, but I wouldn’t stay in my seat, I’d blurt out answers, and I’d lose my homework even when I had actually done it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble managing your emotions, like getting really angry or upset for what seems like no reason?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I get frustrated like anyone, but I haven’t noticed myself getting really angry or upset for no reason in a way that stands out.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of inattention, impulsivity, and restlessness your whole life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it seems like it goes way back. Like I mentioned, my mother still jokes that when I was in grade school my report cards said I was bright, but I wouldn’t stay in my seat, I’d blurt out answers, and I’d lose homework even when I’d actually done it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on something that you lose track of everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I can get focused on things sometimes, but I don’t know that I lose track of everything around me in a way that really stands out.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever become so intensely focused on a task or activity that you tune out everything else around you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can get absorbed in something if I’m interested, but I wouldn’t say I tune everything else out completely or that it happens in some extreme way.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with attention, impulsivity, and restlessness caused you significant problems or stress in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s caused real problems. Since I lost the structure of my old job, things have gotten a lot worse. My wife and I have had to worry about money, too, because people don’t really trust me to get things done when I say I will.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention, impulsivity, or hyperactivity in the past 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s definitely been present in the last six months. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I’m having a really hard time keeping my mind on things long enough to finish them. It’s getting to the point where I can’t just push through it like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Yes. Even when I’m supposed to just sit still and listen, I feel like there’s this motor running under my skin. I’ll want to get up, check something, or move on to the next thing right away. In tech discussions especially, I’ll cut people off even when I know I shouldn’t. The answer pops into my head and I just start talking over them, or sometimes I’ll even reach for the keyboard before they’ve finished explaining the problem. I don’t always stop to think first.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things you're supposed to do during the day?\n\nPatient: Yes, pretty often. I’ll forget normal but important things during the day—like returning a client’s call, paying the cloud backup bill, or bringing the right adapter to a volunteer class. Then I’m stuck the next day apologizing or trying to fix whatever problem I caused.\n\nDoctor: Were you more physically active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes. My mother still jokes about that. She says my grade school report cards always said I was bright, but I wouldn’t stay in my seat, I’d blurt out answers, and I’d lose my homework even when I had actually done it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble managing your emotions, like getting really angry or upset for what seems like no reason?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I get frustrated like anyone, but I haven’t noticed myself getting really angry or upset for no reason in a way that stands out.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of inattention, impulsivity, and restlessness your whole life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it seems like it goes way back. Like I mentioned, my mother still jokes that when I was in grade school my report cards said I was bright, but I wouldn’t stay in my seat, I’d blurt out answers, and I’d lose homework even when I’d actually done it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on something that you lose track of everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I can get focused on things sometimes, but I don’t know that I lose track of everything around me in a way that really stands out.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever become so intensely focused on a task or activity that you tune out everything else around you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can get absorbed in something if I’m interested, but I wouldn’t say I tune everything else out completely or that it happens in some extreme way.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with attention, impulsivity, and restlessness caused you significant problems or stress in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s definitely affected things at home, especially with my wife. Since I lost the structure of my old job, people don’t really trust me to follow through like I say I will. I’ve lost freelance clients, missed out on a senior service-manager role, and that’s put money stress on us. My reputation has taken a real hit, and that spills over into family life.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in the severity of your symptoms since you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it’s been there since I was a kid, just maybe it shows up differently now. It’s been pretty rough.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties in your social relationships, such as with friends, due to these symptoms?\n\nPatient: Yes. With friends too, though I notice it most in work and family. Since I lost the structure of my old job, my reputation has really taken a hit. I’ve lost freelance clients and missed out on a senior service-manager role because people don’t trust me to follow through. And that’s put money stress on me and my wife, which affects how I deal with people generally.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in your life where these symptoms significantly improved or went away for a while?\n\nPatient: Not really went away, no. I think things got a lot worse about a year and ten months ago, after I got fired and lost the routines and systems that were keeping me on track. I mean, I get frustrated like anyone, but I haven’t noticed myself getting really angry or upset for no reason in a way that stands out.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of inattention, impulsivity, and restlessness your whole life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it seems like it goes way back. Like I mentioned, my mother still jokes that when I was in grade school my report cards said I was bright, but I wouldn’t stay in my seat, I’d blurt out answers, and I’d lose homework even when I’d actually done it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on something that you lose track of everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I can get focused on things sometimes, but I don’t know that I lose track of everything around me in a way that really stands out.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever become so intensely focused on a task or activity that you tune out everything else around you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can get absorbed in something if I’m interested, but I wouldn’t say I tune everything else out completely or that it happens in some extreme way.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with attention, impulsivity, and restlessness caused you significant problems or stress in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s caused real problems. Since I lost the structure of my old job, things have gotten a lot worse. My reputation has taken a hit—I’ve lost freelance clients because I didn’t follow through reliably, and I missed out on a senior service-manager role. My wife and I have had to worry about money, too, because people don’t really trust me to get things done when I say I will.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention, impulsivity, or hyperactivity in the past 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s definitely been present in the last six months. Really, it’s been causing serious trouble for about a year and ten months now, since I got fired and lost the systems that used to keep me on track.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties affected your relationships with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s definitely affected things at home, especially with my wife. Since I lost the structure of my old job, people don’t really trust me to follow through like I say I will. I’ve lost freelance clients, missed out on a senior service-manager role, and that’s put money stress on us. My reputation has taken a real hit, and that spills over into family life.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in the severity of your symptoms since you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it’s been there since I was a kid, just maybe it shows up differently now. My mother still jokes that in grade school my report cards always said I was bright, but I wouldn’t stay in my seat, I’d blurt out answers, and I’d lose homework even when I’d actually done it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties due to these symptoms in multiple areas of your life, such as at work, at home, and in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s across different parts of my life. At work, since I lost the structure of my old job, my reputation has really taken a hit. I’ve lost freelance clients and even missed out on a senior service-manager role because people don’t trust me to follow through. And at home, my wife and I have had to worry about money because of all that. It’s been pretty rough.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties in your social relationships, such as with friends, due to these symptoms?\n\nPatient: Yes. With friends too, though I notice it most in work and family. Since I lost the structure of my old job, my reputation has really taken a hit. I’ve lost freelance clients and missed out on a senior service-manager role because people don’t trust me to follow through. And that’s put money stress on me and my wife, which affects how I deal with people generally.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in your life where these symptoms significantly improved or went away for a while?\n\nPatient: Not really went away, no. I think things got a lot worse about a year and ten months ago, after I got fired and lost the routines and systems that were keeping me on track. Before that, it was more contained, I guess, because the structure helped. Since then it’s been causing serious trouble.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms for more than 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s been more than 6 months — about a year and 10 months now, since I was fired and lost the systems that used to keep me on track. That’s when it started causing serious trouble.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

My reputation has taken a real hit, and that spills over into family life.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in the severity of your symptoms since you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it’s been there since I was a kid, just maybe it shows up differently now. My mother still jokes that in grade school my report cards always said I was bright, but I wouldn’t stay in my seat, I’d blurt out answers, and I’d lose homework even when I’d actually done it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties due to these symptoms in multiple areas of your life, such as at work, at home, and in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s across different parts of my life. It’s been pretty rough.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties in your social relationships, such as with friends, due to these symptoms?\n\nPatient: Yes. With friends too, though I notice it most in work and family. Since I lost the structure of my old job, my reputation has really taken a hit. I’ve lost freelance clients and missed out on a senior service-manager role because people don’t trust me to follow through. It’s affected day-to-day life and relationships. Since losing the structure of my old job, my reputation has really suffered. I mean, I get frustrated like anyone, but I haven’t noticed myself getting really angry or upset for no reason in a way that stands out.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of inattention, impulsivity, and restlessness your whole life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it seems like it goes way back. I mean, I get frustrated like anyone, but I haven’t noticed myself getting really angry or upset for no reason in a way that stands out.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of inattention, impulsivity, and restlessness your whole life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it seems like it goes way back. Like I mentioned, my mother still jokes that when I was in grade school my report cards said I was bright, but I wouldn’t stay in my seat, I’d blurt out answers, and I’d lose homework even when I’d actually done it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on something that you lose track of everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I can get focused on things sometimes, but I don’t know that I lose track of everything around me in a way that really stands out.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever become so intensely focused on a task or activity that you tune out everything else around you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no.
